People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.

That’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF05, G31.84, R41.0, R41.1, R41.2, R41.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose I’m here because my thinking hasn’t been quite right. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I could’ve done in my sleep before—and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. It’s been… frustrating.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty with your thinking?\n\nPatient: About three weeks, I think. It started around when my older sister died… or just after that. I’m not trying to make excuses, but that’s when I noticed my thinking getting harder to keep together.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened before your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Before my sister died? I… I don’t think that’s the main issue, doctor. I can remember the larger things well enough, I think. It’s more that now my mind just won’t hold onto things the way it should—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I used to manage without any trouble, the words and numbers just sort of scatter on me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner when speaking?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I hope I’m making sense now. It’s not that I mean to ramble, it’s more that my thoughts don’t stay lined up the way they used to. I’ll try to explain something simple, or write a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers just scatter on me. I used to do that sort of thing without thinking. Now I feel like I’m chasing the thought before it gets away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure of where you are or what time it is?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am, and I’m not getting lost in that way. Time can feel a little blurred sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m confused about it. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to find the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, at least more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but words don’t come together the way they used to. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, and the words and numbers just scatter on me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I didn’t, but that’s really why I came in. My mind just won’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I try to focus, but it slips away. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble learning new things since your thinking started to feel different?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but learning something new feels harder because my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it should. Even with something simple, like writing a note for the youth maker program—something I used to do easily—the words and numbers sort of scatter, and I have to keep starting over. Is that the kind of trouble you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble speaking in complete sentences?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the sense that I can’t make a sentence at all… I think I can, mostly. But I do stumble around more. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, the words and numbers just scatter on me. So I may talk in bits and have to start over. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding things you read?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t hold onto what I’m reading. I’ll look at the words, and a minute later they sort of scatter on me, like with that simple note for the youth maker program I mentioned. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. I’ve let some things fall away—my church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. I kept thinking I’d get back on top of them, but I haven’t.\n\nMy wife has had to step in more, and even my pastor has helped cover things. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop anymore, so I’ve avoided that unless someone’s around. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I can’t do anything, but it has affected things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding other people when they speak to you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're trying to think through something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t know that I’d call them completely disconnected, but when I’m trying to think something through, it just won’t stay together.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I keep trying to gather them back up, but they don’t line up the way they used to.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I know where I am, and I’m not getting lost in that way. Time can feel a little blurred sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m confused about it. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to find the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, at least more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but words don’t come together the way they used to. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, and the words and numbers just scatter on me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I didn’t, but that’s really why I came in. My mind just won’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I try to focus, but it slips away. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble learning new things since your thinking started to feel different?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but learning something new feels harder because my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it should. Even with something simple, like writing a note for the youth maker program—something I used to do easily—the words and numbers sort of scatter, and I have to keep starting over. Is that the kind of trouble you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble speaking in complete sentences?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the sense that I can’t make a sentence at all… I think I can, mostly. But I do stumble around more. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, the words and numbers just scatter on me. So I may talk in bits and have to start over. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding things you read?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t hold onto what I’m reading. I’ll look at the words, and a minute later they sort of scatter on me, like with that simple note for the youth maker program I mentioned. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. I’ve let some things fall away—my church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. I kept thinking I’d get back on top of them, but I haven’t.\n\nMy wife has had to step in more, and even my pastor has helped cover things. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop anymore, so I’ve avoided that unless someone’s around. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I can’t do anything, but it has affected things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding other people when they speak to you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're trying to think through something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t know that I’d call them completely disconnected, but when I’m trying to think something through, it just won’t stay together.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I keep trying to gather them back up, but they don’t line up the way they used to.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have noticed a change. I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.\n\nEven when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently, trying to help… it can feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well, if that makes sense. I can hear her, and I know she means well, but I still feel very far down. Is that the sort of mood change you’re asking about?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Even with something simple, like writing a note for the youth maker program—something I used to do easily—the words and numbers sort of scatter, and I have to keep starting over. Is that the kind of trouble you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble speaking in complete sentences?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the sense that I can’t make a sentence at all… I think I can, mostly. But I do stumble around more. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, the words and numbers just scatter on me. So I may talk in bits and have to start over. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding things you read?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t hold onto what I’m reading. I’ll look at the words, and a minute later they sort of scatter on me, like with that simple note for the youth maker program I mentioned. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. I’ve let some things fall away—my church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. I kept thinking I’d get back on top of them, but I haven’t.\n\nMy wife has had to step in more, and even my pastor has helped cover things. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop anymore, so I’ve avoided that unless someone’s around. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I can’t do anything, but it has affected things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding other people when they speak to you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're trying to think through something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t know that I’d call them completely disconnected, but when I’m trying to think something through, it just won’t stay together.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I keep trying to gather them back up, but they don’t line up the way they used to.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have noticed a change. I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.\n\nEven when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently, trying to help… it can feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well, if that makes sense. I can hear her, and I know she means well, but I still feel very far down. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. Even with something simple, like writing a note for the youth maker program—something I used to do easily—the words and numbers sort of scatter, and I have to keep starting over. Is that the kind of trouble you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble speaking in complete sentences?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the sense that I can’t make a sentence at all… I think I can, mostly. But I do stumble around more. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, the words and numbers just scatter on me. So I may talk in bits and have to start over. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding things you read?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t hold onto what I’m reading. I’ll look at the words, and a minute later they sort of scatter on me, like with that simple note for the youth maker program I mentioned. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. I’ve let some things fall away—my church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. I kept thinking I’d get back on top of them, but I haven’t.\n\nMy wife has had to step in more, and even my pastor has helped cover things. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop anymore, so I’ve avoided that unless someone’s around. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I can’t do anything, but it has affected things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding other people when they speak to you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're trying to think through something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t know that I’d call them completely disconnected, but when I’m trying to think something through, it just won’t stay together.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I keep trying to gather them back up, but they don’t line up the way they used to.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have noticed a change. I can hear her, and I know she means well, but I still feel very far down. Is that the sort of mood change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt since your sister's death?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. Maybe more like… I keep questioning myself. But I’m not sure I’d put it as strongly as worthlessness.\n\nIs that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that aren't there since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I’ll try to explain something simple, or write a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers just scatter on me. I used to do that sort of thing without thinking. Now I feel like I’m chasing the thought before it gets away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disoriented or unsure of where you are or what time it is?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I know where I am, and I’m not getting lost in that way. Time can feel a little blurred sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’m confused about it. Is that… is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to find the right words when speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I do, at least more than I’d like to admit. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but words don’t come together the way they used to. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, and the words and numbers just scatter on me. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I didn’t, but that’s really why I came in. My mind just won’t seem to hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I try to focus, but it slips away. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble learning new things since your thinking started to feel different?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t want to overstate it, but learning something new feels harder because my mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it should. Even with something simple, like writing a note for the youth maker program—something I used to do easily—the words and numbers sort of scatter, and I have to keep starting over. Is that the kind of trouble you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble speaking in complete sentences?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the sense that I can’t make a sentence at all… I think I can, mostly. But I do stumble around more. I came in because my mind just won’t hold onto things anymore—like when I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done in my sleep before, the words and numbers just scatter on me. So I may talk in bits and have to start over. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding things you read?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t hold onto what I’m reading. I’ll look at the words, and a minute later they sort of scatter on me, like with that simple note for the youth maker program I mentioned. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. I’ve let some things fall away—my church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. I kept thinking I’d get back on top of them, but I haven’t.\n\nMy wife has had to step in more, and even my pastor has helped cover things. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop anymore, so I’ve avoided that unless someone’s around. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I can’t do anything, but it has affected things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding other people when they speak to you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're trying to think through something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t know that I’d call them completely disconnected, but when I’m trying to think something through, it just won’t stay together.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I keep trying to gather them back up, but they don’t line up the way they used to.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have noticed a change. I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.\n\nEven when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently, trying to help… it can feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well, if that makes sense. I can hear her, and I know she means well, but I still feel very far down. Is that the sort of mood change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt since your sister's death?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t want to say something that sounds harsher than it is.\n\nI do feel bad sometimes, of course, and I wonder if I should have handled things differently, but I don’t know if that’s the same as “worthless” or “guilty” the way you mean it. Maybe more like… I keep questioning myself. But I’m not sure I’d put it as strongly as worthlessness.\n\nIs that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that aren't there since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think about her so strongly it almost feels like she’s close by, but I haven’t been seeing her or hearing voices or anything like that.\n\nAt least, not in the way I think you mean. Is that… is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when talking or thinking about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s actually one of the reasons I came in.\n\nMy mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and then the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I look at the page and lose the thread.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean by keeping my thoughts in order, but… yes, it has been hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another unrelated idea when thinking or talking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of jumping to unrelated things, at least. I may lose my place or have to pause, but I’m not usually going off from one topic to something completely different.\n\nIf I do that a little here, please tell me—I’m trying to answer clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your overall ability to think and remember has declined significantly since your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has. I don’t like saying “significantly,” because that makes it sound so dramatic, but… that is part of why I came in.\n\nMy mind just doesn’t hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before—and then the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me.\n\nI’m trying not to make too much of it, but yes… it feels like a real decline. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think so. I’m aware that I’m here with you, and I’m following what you’re asking.

I’ll try to explain something simple, or write a note for the youth maker program, and the words and numbers just scatter on me. I used to do that sort of thing without thinking. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I do. I wish I didn’t, but that’s really why I came in. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble learning new things since your thinking started to feel different?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Is that the kind of trouble you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble speaking in complete sentences?\n\nPatient: Not usually in the sense that I can’t make a sentence at all… I think I can, mostly. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding things you read?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I do, though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more that my mind just doesn’t hold onto what I’m reading. I’ll look at the words, and a minute later they sort of scatter on me, like with that simple note for the youth maker program I mentioned. It used to be easy, but now the words and numbers don’t stay put. Is that answering it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Has your difficulty with thinking and memory affected your ability to perform daily tasks or maintain your independence?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it has, more than I like to admit. I’ve let some things fall away—my church responsibilities, the garden, and the youth maker program. I kept thinking I’d get back on top of them, but I haven’t.\n\nMy wife has had to step in more, and even my pastor has helped cover things. And… I don’t really feel safe being by myself in the workshop anymore, so I’ve avoided that unless someone’s around. I hate saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I can’t do anything, but it has affected things. Is that the kind of impact you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble understanding other people when they speak to you?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t think I completely misunderstand people most of the time. It’s more like my mind doesn’t hold onto what’s being said. Someone can tell me something simple, and I’m trying to follow, but the words don’t stay together the way they used to.\n\nThat’s part of why I came in—my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. Even sitting down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I could’ve done easily before, the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. So when people talk, I may need them to repeat it or I have to piece it back together. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're trying to think through something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I don’t know that I’d call them completely disconnected, but when I’m trying to think something through, it just won’t stay together.\n\nThat’s really why I came in—my mind just doesn’t hold onto things anymore. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program, something I honestly could’ve done in my sleep before, and the numbers and words sort of scatter on me. I keep trying to gather them back up, but they don’t line up the way they used to.\n\nI hope that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your mood since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have noticed a change. I don’t want to make it sound overly dramatic, but most days I wake up with this heavy, hollow feeling in my chest.\n\nEven when my wife is being kind to me, talking gently, trying to help… it can feel like I’m sitting at the bottom of a well, if that makes sense. I can hear her, and I know she means well, but I still feel very far down. Is that the sort of mood change you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing feelings of worthlessness or guilt since your sister's death?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I wouldn’t want to say something that sounds harsher than it is.\n\nI do feel bad sometimes, of course, and I wonder if I should have handled things differently, but I don’t know if that’s the same as “worthless” or “guilty” the way you mean it. Maybe more like… I keep questioning myself. But I’m not sure I’d put it as strongly as worthlessness.\n\nIs that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you been hearing or seeing things that aren't there since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve had moments where I think about her so strongly it almost feels like she’s close by, but I haven’t been seeing her or hearing voices or anything like that.\n\nAt least, not in the way I think you mean. Is that… is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep your thoughts in order when talking or thinking about something?\n\nPatient: Yes… a bit. I don’t want to overstate it, but that’s actually one of the reasons I came in.\n\nMy mind just doesn’t seem to hold onto things the way it used to. I’ll sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program—something I used to be able to do almost without thinking—and then the numbers and words just sort of scatter on me. I look at the page and lose the thread.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean by keeping my thoughts in order, but… yes, it has been hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another unrelated idea when thinking or talking?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of jumping to unrelated things, at least. I may lose my place or have to pause, but I’m not usually going off from one topic to something completely different.\n\nIf I do that a little here, please tell me—I’m trying to answer clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your overall ability to think and remember has declined significantly since your sister's death?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it has.
